========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. Smith. Well, I guess what brings me in is... I just keep messing up these little things. It's getting kind of frustrating. Like, the other day I was filling out a sign-up sheet for a class at the senior center, and I ended up writing down the completely wrong time. 

It was for a woodworking class, actually. My grandfather used to do a lot of woodworking—he built this incredible cedar shed back in the 70s, completely from scratch. Cedar is great wood, you know? Smells wonderful, keeps all the bugs and moths away. I always wanted to build something like that myself, maybe a nice birdhouse or a bench... but anyway, what was I saying? 

Oh, right, the class. So I'm finally there, and I'm measuring a board, and I swear I looked at it, but I still cut it a hair short. I just sort of skipped over the small print on the plans. The saws they have at the center aren't the best, either, nothing like the old Makita I used to have before I downsized my garage, but still... I just keep making these silly little mistakes like that.

Oh, it's definitely not a new thing. I think I've always been like this. I actually was just looking through some boxes in the attic a few months ago, and I found this old tin my mother kept. It was one of those blue Danish butter cookie tins, you know the ones? I used to love those cookies as a kid. Though half the time you'd open one at my grandmother's house and it would just be filled with sewing supplies—needles, thread, buttons. Always such a disappointment when you were hoping for a sugar cookie. 

But anyway, my mother used this particular tin to keep all my old elementary school report cards. And reading through them, it was funny, every single teacher wrote the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright boy, but I constantly forgot my books, or I'd somehow completely lose my homework. And Mrs. Gable—my fourth-grade teacher—she specifically wrote that I'd just stare out the window all day unless someone actively kept me on track. There was this massive old oak tree right outside her classroom window, and I'd just watch the squirrels chasing each other around the branches for God knows how long... so, yeah, I guess I've always been a bit like this.

Well, yeah, it definitely bleeds into other things. It's not just the woodworking. In fact, the mix-ups have gotten bad enough that I actually had to step back from helping lead a couple of DIY workshops at the center. I just couldn't keep the schedules and the supply lists straight anymore. Which is a shame, because I really enjoyed teaching people how to use a router properly. You'd be amazed how many folks try to force a router through the wood instead of letting the bit do the work. It just ruins the edge, and sometimes it burns the wood—especially pine. Pine burns so easily if you aren't careful. 

But, uh, anyway, yeah, it causes problems at home, too. My wife, Mary, she's basically started double-checking all our plans now. Because if she doesn't, I'll end up throwing off our family visits or mixing up the times for other activities at the center. Like last month, I told my daughter we'd be over for Sunday dinner at 4:00, but I had completely forgotten Mary already promised we'd help with the center's bake sale until 5:00. Mary makes these incredible lemon bars for those sales, by the way. Real lemon juice, none of that extract nonsense. But yeah, she has to check my calendar constantly now so I don't mess things up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I wouldn't say I'm exactly blurting things out, but... I do get restless, I suppose. Sitting through a whole meeting or, you know, just a quiet evening at the condo, I get this ants-in-my-pants feeling. I just want to get up, check something, or putter around. 

We moved to the condo a few years ago, actually. It's a nice place, mostly quiet, except for the guy upstairs who insists on vacuuming at 6 AM on Saturdays. I don't know what kind of vacuum he has, but it sounds like a jet engine. My old shop vac was quieter than that, and that thing could suck up a two-by-four if you weren't careful. I miss having a real garage to putter around in, honestly. The condo association is so strict about what you can leave on the balcony. I tried to set up a little workbench out there, just a small folding one, but they sent me a letter about it within three days. Three days! But anyway, yeah, I just get that fidgety feeling where I need to be moving around doing something.

No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or anxious, really. I mean, I don't lay awake worrying about things or feeling down in the dumps. Life is pretty good, all things considered. The craftsmanship back then was just... well, they don't make them like that anymore, that's for sure. Now everything is just cheap plastic stamped out in a factory somewhere. You cast it twice and the paint chips off. But no, to answer your question, no real sadness or anxiety or anything like that. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, the restless part... not so much as a kid, I don't think. I wasn't really the type to be bouncing off the walls or running around the classroom. But the forgetfulness and the spacing out? Oh, absolutely. Even way back in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was always forgetting my textbooks, losing my homework, that sort of thing. I'd just sit there staring out the window unless the teacher specifically called on me to keep me on track. 

I remember my fifth-grade classroom had these big, beautiful oak windows. Real solid wood, you know? The kind they used to put in those old brick schoolhouses before they started replacing everything with aluminum frames that leak cold air all winter. Anyway, right outside my window was this massive elm tree. This was before Dutch elm disease wiped most of them out in our area. It was a shame, really. They had to cut down almost the whole street of them. The city came by with these huge chainsaws, and it took them three days just to clear the block. The smell of fresh-cut elm is actually pretty distinct—kind of sour, not sweet like cedar or pine. But yeah, I'd just sit there looking at the branches swaying, totally losing track of whatever we were supposed to be learning about... fractions, probably. I always hated fractions until I started woodworking. Then you have to know your fractions, or you'll cut a board a quarter-inch too short and ruin the whole piece. But yeah, the teachers always said I was in my own little world back then.

Smells wonderful, keeps all the bugs and moths away. I always wanted to build something like that myself, maybe a nice birdhouse or a bench... but anyway, what was I saying? \n\nOh, right, the class. So I'm finally there, and I'm measuring a board, and I swear I looked at it, but I still cut it a hair short. I just sort of skipped over the small print on the plans. The saws they have at the center aren't the best, either, nothing like the old Makita I used to have before I downsized my garage, but still... I just keep making these silly little mistakes like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been someone who tends to make these kinds of small mistakes, even back in school or earlier adulthood, or is this something new that's developed more recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely not a new thing. I think I've always been like this. I actually was just looking through some boxes in the attic a few months ago, and I found this old tin my mother kept. It was one of those blue Danish butter cookie tins, you know the ones? I used to love those cookies as a kid. Though half the time you'd open one at my grandmother's house and it would just be filled with sewing supplies—needles, thread, buttons. Always such a disappointment when you were hoping for a sugar cookie. \n\nBut anyway, my mother used this particular tin to keep all my old elementary school report cards. And reading through them, it was funny, every single teacher wrote the exact same thing. They'd say I was a bright boy, but I constantly forgot my books, or I'd somehow completely lose my homework. And Mrs. Gable—my fourth-grade teacher—she specifically wrote that I'd just stare out the window all day unless someone actively kept me on track. There was this massive old oak tree right outside her classroom window, and I'd just watch the squirrels chasing each other around the branches for God knows how long... so, yeah, I guess I've always been a bit like this.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these specific incidents, would you say these kinds of attention slips and forgetfulness currently cause noticeable problems in multiple areas of your life—like at home, in relationships, or with other responsibilities—not just during specific tasks like the woodworking class?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it definitely bleeds into other things. It's not just the woodworking. In fact, the mix-ups have gotten bad enough that I actually had to step back from helping lead a couple of DIY workshops at the center. I just couldn't keep the schedules and the supply lists straight anymore. Which is a shame, because I really enjoyed teaching people how to use a router properly. You'd be amazed how many folks try to force a router through the wood instead of letting the bit do the work. It just ruins the edge, and sometimes it burns the wood—especially pine. Pine burns so easily if you aren't careful. \n\nBut, uh, anyway, yeah, it causes problems at home, too. My wife, Mary, she's basically started double-checking all our plans now. Because if she doesn't, I'll end up throwing off our family visits or mixing up the times for other activities at the center. Like last month, I told my daughter we'd be over for Sunday dinner at 4:00, but I had completely forgotten Mary already promised we'd help with the center's bake sale until 5:00. Mary makes these incredible lemon bars for those sales, by the way. Real lemon juice, none of that extract nonsense. But yeah, she has to check my calendar constantly now so I don't mess things up.\n\nDoctor: Besides these attention and forgetfulness issues, have you ever noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still, or acting impulsively—like blurting things out or making quick decisions without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm exactly blurting things out, but... I do get restless, I suppose. Sitting through a whole meeting or, you know, just a quiet evening at the condo, I get this ants-in-my-pants feeling. I just want to get up, check something, or putter around. \n\nWe moved to the condo a few years ago, actually. It's a nice place, mostly quiet, except for the guy upstairs who insists on vacuuming at 6 AM on Saturdays. I don't know what kind of vacuum he has, but it sounds like a jet engine. My old shop vac was quieter than that, and that thing could suck up a two-by-four if you weren't careful. I miss having a real garage to putter around in, honestly. The condo association is so strict about what you can leave on the balcony. I tried to set up a little workbench out there, just a small folding one, but they sent me a letter about it within three days. Three days! But anyway, yeah, I just get that fidgety feeling where I need to be moving around doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, worry, or anxiety recently that might be affecting your concentration or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or anxious, really. I mean, I don't lay awake worrying about things or feeling down in the dumps. Life is pretty good, all things considered. Mary and I are enjoying retirement, mostly. We did take a trip down to Florida last winter—Naples, specifically. Beautiful down there, though the humidity is something else. You step outside and your glasses fog up instantly. We stayed at this little rental place right near the beach, and the owner had this incredible collection of vintage fishing lures mounted on the wall in the living room. Hand-painted, wooden ones from the 1930s. I spent an hour just looking at how they carved the gills on those things. The craftsmanship back then was just... well, they don't make them like that anymore, that's for sure. Now everything is just cheap plastic stamped out in a factory somewhere. You cast it twice and the paint chips off. But no, to answer your question, no real sadness or anxiety or anything like that. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid, do you remember having that same restless, need-to-be-moving feeling, like having trouble staying seated in your classroom?\n\nPatient: Well, the restless part... not so much as a kid, I don't think. I wasn't really the type to be bouncing off the walls or running around the classroom. But the forgetfulness and the spacing out? Oh, absolutely. Even way back in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was always forgetting my textbooks, losing my homework, that sort of thing. I'd just sit there staring out the window unless the teacher specifically called on me to keep me on track. \n\nI remember my fifth-grade classroom had these big, beautiful oak windows. Real solid wood, you know? The kind they used to put in those old brick schoolhouses before they started replacing everything with aluminum frames that leak cold air all winter. Anyway, right outside my window was this massive elm tree. This was before Dutch elm disease wiped most of them out in our area. It was a shame, really. They had to cut down almost the whole street of them. The city came by with these huge chainsaws, and it took them three days just to clear the block. The smell of fresh-cut elm is actually pretty distinct—kind of sour, not sweet like cedar or pine. But yeah, I'd just sit there looking at the branches swaying, totally losing track of whatever we were supposed to be learning about... fractions, probably. I always hated fractions until I started woodworking. Then you have to know your fractions, or you'll cut a board a quarter-inch too short and ruin the whole piece. But yeah, the teachers always said I was in my own little world back then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

Interrupting? No, I don't think so. I'm usually the quiet one just listening, to be honest. Mary does enough talking for the both of us, bless her heart. And waiting in lines... well, I don't really mind it. I'm retired now, so it's not like I'm in a rush to get anywhere.

I just stood there for a good twenty minutes looking at the macaroni salad in the glass case. They put way too much mayonnaise in it nowadays, it's just swimming in it. I tried to set up a little workbench out there, just a small folding one, but they sent me a letter about it within three days. Three days! But anyway, yeah, I just get that fidgety feeling where I need to be moving around doing something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of sadness, worry, or anxiety recently that might be affecting your concentration or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad or anxious, really. I mean, I don't lay awake worrying about things or feeling down in the dumps. Life is pretty good, all things considered. Mary and I are enjoying retirement, mostly. We did take a trip down to Florida last winter—Naples, specifically. Beautiful down there, though the humidity is something else. You step outside and your glasses fog up instantly. We stayed at this little rental place right near the beach, and the owner had this incredible collection of vintage fishing lures mounted on the wall in the living room. Hand-painted, wooden ones from the 1930s. I spent an hour just looking at how they carved the gills on those things. The craftsmanship back then was just... well, they don't make them like that anymore, that's for sure. Now everything is just cheap plastic stamped out in a factory somewhere. You cast it twice and the paint chips off. But no, to answer your question, no real sadness or anxiety or anything like that. Just the usual day-to-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were a kid, do you remember having that same restless, need-to-be-moving feeling, like having trouble staying seated in your classroom?\n\nPatient: Well, the restless part... not so much as a kid, I don't think. I wasn't really the type to be bouncing off the walls or running around the classroom. But the forgetfulness and the spacing out? Oh, absolutely. Even way back in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was always forgetting my textbooks, losing my homework, that sort of thing. I'd just sit there staring out the window unless the teacher specifically called on me to keep me on track. \n\nI remember my fifth-grade classroom had these big, beautiful oak windows. Real solid wood, you know? The kind they used to put in those old brick schoolhouses before they started replacing everything with aluminum frames that leak cold air all winter. Anyway, right outside my window was this massive elm tree. This was before Dutch elm disease wiped most of them out in our area. It was a shame, really. They had to cut down almost the whole street of them. The city came by with these huge chainsaws, and it took them three days just to clear the block. The smell of fresh-cut elm is actually pretty distinct—kind of sour, not sweet like cedar or pine. But yeah, I'd just sit there looking at the branches swaying, totally losing track of whatever we were supposed to be learning about... fractions, probably. I always hated fractions until I started woodworking. Then you have to know your fractions, or you'll cut a board a quarter-inch too short and ruin the whole piece. But yeah, the teachers always said I was in my own little world back then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting conversations, finishing other people's sentences, or having trouble waiting your turn in lines or discussions?\n\nPatient: Interrupting? No, I don't think so. I'm usually the quiet one just listening, to be honest. Mary does enough talking for the both of us, bless her heart. And waiting in lines... well, I don't really mind it. I'm retired now, so it's not like I'm in a rush to get anywhere. \n\nThough I will say, the lines at the grocery store lately have been something else. They put in all those new self-checkout machines. Have you tried using those things? Absolute nightmare. \"Unexpected item in the bagging area.\" It yells it at you every single time! I just want to buy a couple of tomatoes and maybe a block of that sharp cheddar cheese. The deli counter is even worse, actually. You have to pull one of those little paper tickets. Last Tuesday I pulled a 42 and the red sign said they were only serving 18. I just stood there for a good twenty minutes looking at the macaroni salad in the glass case. They put way too much mayonnaise in it nowadays, it's just swimming in it. My mother used to make macaroni salad with just a touch of yellow mustard and some finely diced celery. Gave it a real nice crunch, you know? You don't get that crunch from the store-bought stuff. \n\nBut anyway, what were we saying? Oh, right. No, I don't really get impatient or jump into people's sentences. I just kind of stand there and wait my turn.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. We did take a trip down to Florida last winter—Naples, specifically. Beautiful down there, though the humidity is something else. You step outside and your glasses fog up instantly. We stayed at this little rental place right near the beach, and the owner had this incredible collection of vintage fishing lures mounted on the wall in the living room. Hand-painted, wooden ones from the 1930s. I spent an hour just looking at how they carved the gills on those things. The craftsmanship back then was just... well, they don't make them like that anymore, that's for sure. Now everything is just cheap plastic stamped out in a factory somewhere. The smell of fresh-cut elm is actually pretty distinct—kind of sour, not sweet like cedar or pine.
